People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Honestly, the biggest thing is that I get pulled off track really easily. Like, I’ll be working on something I’m supposed to finish—usually a dashboard or some kind of project—and then one tiny thing happens, like a Slack notification pops up, or I hear one line of a song, or I suddenly think of a completely different project idea, and it’s like my brain just… switches channels.

And then I’ll realize I’ve totally lost the thread of what I was doing. I’ll be sitting there with the dashboard open, but I don’t remember exactly what part I was fixing or what I was about to check next. It’s frustrating because it’s not like I’m trying to avoid it; I really do mean to stay on task. It just feels like my attention gets grabbed before I can stop it, and then getting back into the original thing takes way more effort than it should. That’s kind of what made me think I should talk to someone.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I think so. I mean, looking back, it was definitely there even when I was little. I didn’t really have words for it then, and I think adults kind of framed it as me being “careless” or “too chatty” or needing to slow down.

In elementary school, my teachers would write those little report card comments like, “rushes through worksheets,” or “needs to follow directions more carefully,” and I remember getting frustrated because I usually thought I did understand the directions at first. But then I’d start the worksheet too fast, skip a step, or forget partway through what we were supposed to be doing. Sometimes I’d finish something and then realize I’d done, like, the wrong section or missed the part where we were supposed to show work or answer in complete sentences.

And with group work, I apparently talked over people a lot. Teachers would mention that too—like I needed to let classmates finish or take turns better. I wasn’t trying to be rude, I just would get excited or have a thought and jump in before I lost it.

Homework was a whole thing at home too. My parents used to literally sit next to me while I did it, not necessarily to help with the answers, but to keep me there. If they walked away, I’d drift off and sharpen pencils, or start drawing in the margins, or suddenly decide I needed to organize something, or just wander into another room before I’d actually finished. So yes, it was pretty similar, just in a kid version.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, it does. I mean, I can usually sort of hold things together enough that it doesn’t look catastrophic from the outside, but it definitely affects more than just one part of my life.

Work-wise—or I guess freelance-wise, since I’m not in a regular job right now—I’ve lost a couple of small contracts because I didn’t follow through cleanly. Like, I had good ideas and I could do the creative or technical parts, but then I’d miss a message, or forget to send a revision when I said I would, or turn something in kind of messy because I’d lost track of the final details. And once that happens, people don’t really want to keep relying on you, even if they’re nice about it.

There was also a portfolio competition I really wanted to enter, and I missed out on it because I couldn’t get everything organized and submitted properly. That one really bothered me because it wasn’t that I didn’t care. I cared a lot. I just couldn’t seem to make the follow-through happen in a clean, dependable way.

And socially, it affects collaborations and friendships too. Some friends still like my ideas and like brainstorming with me, but I can tell they hesitate to actually work with me on things because they’re not sure I’ll remember what I said I’d do or finish my part smoothly. That feels pretty embarrassing, honestly, because I don’t want to be that person people have to manage.

Um… I don’t think so? Not in the way you’re describing, at least. That feels pretty embarrassing, honestly, because I don’t want to be that person people have to manage.\n\nDoctor: Over the past few months, have you been feeling excessively worried or tense on most days, even when nothing specific is going wrong?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? I don’t think so, generally. Like, I don’t usually feel confused by people’s facial expressions or totally miss when someone is joking or bored. I can usually tell, at least in a normal way, if someone’s uncomfortable or if the tone of a conversation changes.

I mean, I might sometimes keep talking too long and then realize a little late that I should wrap it up, but that feels more like I got carried away than like I didn’t understand the cue at all. If someone is being sarcastic or making a joke, I usually get it. And I don’t think I’ve had a pattern of people saying I don’t understand what’s expected socially.

So… no, I wouldn’t say that’s been a major issue for me.

I mean, I can usually sort of hold things together enough that it doesn’t look catastrophic from the outside, but it definitely affects more than just one part of my life.\n\nWork-wise—or I guess freelance-wise, since I’m not in a regular job right now—I’ve lost a couple of small contracts because I didn’t follow through cleanly. Like, I had good ideas and I could do the creative or technical parts, but then I’d miss a message, or forget to send a revision when I said I would, or turn something in kind of messy because I’d lost track of the final details. And once that happens, people don’t really want to keep relying on you, even if they’re nice about it.\n\nThere was also a portfolio competition I really wanted to enter, and I missed out on it because I couldn’t get everything organized and submitted properly. That one really bothered me because it wasn’t that I didn’t care. I cared a lot. I just couldn’t seem to make the follow-through happen in a clean, dependable way.\n\nAnd socially, it affects collaborations and friendships too. Some friends still like my ideas and like brainstorming with me, but I can tell they hesitate to actually work with me on things because they’re not sure I’ll remember what I said I’d do or finish my part smoothly. That feels pretty embarrassing, honestly, because I don’t want to be that person people have to manage.\n\nDoctor: Over the past few months, have you been feeling excessively worried or tense on most days, even when nothing specific is going wrong?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in the way you’re describing, at least. I mean, I do get stressed when I’m behind on something or when I realize I dropped the ball, and then I can kind of spiral a little about the consequences. But it doesn’t feel like I’m just tense or worried most days for no clear reason.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m walking around constantly anxious when nothing is happening. It’s more like the worry shows up when there’s an actual thing I’m dealing with or something I forgot to deal with. If everything is relatively okay, I don’t usually feel excessively worried just out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Over the last few weeks, have you been feeling down, sad, or noticeably less interested or less motivated to do things than is usual for you?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t think I’ve been, like, persistently sad or down in a way that feels unusual for me. I have moments where I feel frustrated or kind of discouraged, especially when I’m not getting things done the way I want to, but I wouldn’t say the last few weeks have felt like a constant low mood.\n\nAs for interest or motivation… that’s a little harder, because my motivation can be kind of inconsistent anyway, so it’s hard for me to tell what’s “less than usual.” I don’t think I’ve noticed a clear drop where suddenly I don’t care about anything or nothing feels worth doing. It’s more that I can get stuck or have trouble starting, but I don’t know if I’d describe that as feeling depressed.\n\nDoctor: Outside of being talkative or interrupting, have you generally found it hard to understand or keep up with social cues—like reading people’s expressions, knowing when someone is bored or joking, or figuring out what’s expected in conversations?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, generally. Like, I don’t usually feel confused by people’s facial expressions or totally miss when someone is joking or bored. I can usually tell, at least in a normal way, if someone’s uncomfortable or if the tone of a conversation changes.\n\nI mean, I might sometimes keep talking too long and then realize a little late that I should wrap it up, but that feels more like I got carried away than like I didn’t understand the cue at all. If someone is being sarcastic or making a joke, I usually get it. And I don’t think I’ve had a pattern of people saying I don’t understand what’s expected socially.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say that’s been a major issue for me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

That’s kind of what made me think I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back to when you were a kid in elementary or middle school, did you often have similar trouble staying focused on schoolwork or instructions, even for things you were trying to pay attention to?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, looking back, it was definitely there even when I was little. I didn’t really have words for it then, and I think adults kind of framed it as me being “careless” or “too chatty” or needing to slow down.\n\nIn elementary school, my teachers would write those little report card comments like, “rushes through worksheets,” or “needs to follow directions more carefully,” and I remember getting frustrated because I usually thought I did understand the directions at first. But then I’d start the worksheet too fast, skip a step, or forget partway through what we were supposed to be doing. Sometimes I’d finish something and then realize I’d done, like, the wrong section or missed the part where we were supposed to show work or answer in complete sentences.\n\nAnd with group work, I apparently talked over people a lot. Teachers would mention that too—like I needed to let classmates finish or take turns better. I wasn’t trying to be rude, I just would get excited or have a thought and jump in before I lost it.\n\nHomework was a whole thing at home too. My parents used to literally sit next to me while I did it, not necessarily to help with the answers, but to keep me there. If they walked away, I’d drift off and sharpen pencils, or start drawing in the margins, or suddenly decide I needed to organize something, or just wander into another room before I’d actually finished. So yes, it was pretty similar, just in a kid version.\n\nDoctor: These attention problems you’re describing now—do they significantly get in the way of your day-to-day functioning in more than one area of your life, like at work, at home, or in relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. I mean, I can usually sort of hold things together enough that it doesn’t look catastrophic from the outside, but it definitely affects more than just one part of my life.\n\nWork-wise—or I guess freelance-wise, since I’m not in a regular job right now—I’ve lost a couple of small contracts because I didn’t follow through cleanly. Like, I had good ideas and I could do the creative or technical parts, but then I’d miss a message, or forget to send a revision when I said I would, or turn something in kind of messy because I’d lost track of the final details. And once that happens, people don’t really want to keep relying on you, even if they’re nice about it.\n\nThere was also a portfolio competition I really wanted to enter, and I missed out on it because I couldn’t get everything organized and submitted properly. That one really bothered me because it wasn’t that I didn’t care. I cared a lot. I just couldn’t seem to make the follow-through happen in a clean, dependable way.\n\nAnd socially, it affects collaborations and friendships too. Some friends still like my ideas and like brainstorming with me, but I can tell they hesitate to actually work with me on things because they’re not sure I’ll remember what I said I’d do or finish my part smoothly. That feels pretty embarrassing, honestly, because I don’t want to be that person people have to manage.\n\nDoctor: Over the past few months, have you been feeling excessively worried or tense on most days, even when nothing specific is going wrong?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in the way you’re describing, at least. I mean, I do get stressed when I’m behind on something or when I realize I dropped the ball, and then I can kind of spiral a little about the consequences. But it doesn’t feel like I’m just tense or worried most days for no clear reason.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m walking around constantly anxious when nothing is happening. It’s more like the worry shows up when there’s an actual thing I’m dealing with or something I forgot to deal with. If everything is relatively okay, I don’t usually feel excessively worried just out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Over the last few weeks, have you been feeling down, sad, or noticeably less interested or less motivated to do things than is usual for you?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t think I’ve been, like, persistently sad or down in a way that feels unusual for me. I have moments where I feel frustrated or kind of discouraged, especially when I’m not getting things done the way I want to, but I wouldn’t say the last few weeks have felt like a constant low mood.\n\nAs for interest or motivation… that’s a little harder, because my motivation can be kind of inconsistent anyway, so it’s hard for me to tell what’s “less than usual.” I don’t think I’ve noticed a clear drop where suddenly I don’t care about anything or nothing feels worth doing. It’s more that I can get stuck or have trouble starting, but I don’t know if I’d describe that as feeling depressed.\n\nDoctor: Outside of being talkative or interrupting, have you generally found it hard to understand or keep up with social cues—like reading people’s expressions, knowing when someone is bored or joking, or figuring out what’s expected in conversations?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, generally. Like, I don’t usually feel confused by people’s facial expressions or totally miss when someone is joking or bored. I can usually tell, at least in a normal way, if someone’s uncomfortable or if the tone of a conversation changes.\n\nI mean, I might sometimes keep talking too long and then realize a little late that I should wrap it up, but that feels more like I got carried away than like I didn’t understand the cue at all. If someone is being sarcastic or making a joke, I usually get it. And I don’t think I’ve had a pattern of people saying I don’t understand what’s expected socially.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say that’s been a major issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble falling asleep at night, taking a long time to drift off even when you’re trying to sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I definitely have nights where it takes me a while to fall asleep, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s “often” in a way that feels like a main problem. Sometimes I get into bed and I’m trying to sleep, but my mind is still kind of… going through little things, or I’m just not feeling ready to fully shut off yet.\n\nBut it’s not like every night I’m lying there for hours feeling unable to sleep. I haven’t really tracked it, so it’s hard for me to say how frequent it is. I guess I’d say it happens sometimes, but I’m not sure it’s a consistent pattern or something I’ve paid close attention to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"candidates": ["F90.0", "F32.0", "F41.0", "F43.2", "F51.0"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I think so. That’s actually one of the things I notice more clearly. If I’m supposed to just sit still through something, like a long Zoom call or when I’m trying to quietly work in one place for a while, I start getting this really uncomfortable feeling in my body. It’s not exactly pain, it’s more like… pressure, or tension, like I need to get up and move around or do something.

Sometimes I’ll start shifting positions a lot, or I’ll want to pace for a minute, or I’ll end up messing with something in my hands just to have somewhere for that energy to go. It’s not always that I actually leave the situation, especially if I know I’m supposed to stay put, but internally I feel like I’m kind of fighting the urge to move. It can make sitting through things feel way longer than they probably are.

I’ll be sitting there with the dashboard open, but I don’t remember exactly what part I was fixing or what I was about to check next. It’s frustrating because it’s not like I’m trying to avoid it; I really do mean to stay on task. It just feels like my attention gets grabbed before I can stop it, and then getting back into the original thing takes way more effort than it should. That’s kind of what made me think I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back to when you were a kid in elementary or middle school, did you often have similar trouble staying focused on schoolwork or instructions, even for things you were trying to pay attention to?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, looking back, it was definitely there even when I was little. I didn’t really have words for it then, and I think adults kind of framed it as me being “careless” or “too chatty” or needing to slow down.\n\nIn elementary school, my teachers would write those little report card comments like, “rushes through worksheets,” or “needs to follow directions more carefully,” and I remember getting frustrated because I usually thought I did understand the directions at first. But then I’d start the worksheet too fast, skip a step, or forget partway through what we were supposed to be doing. Sometimes I’d finish something and then realize I’d done, like, the wrong section or missed the part where we were supposed to show work or answer in complete sentences.\n\nAnd with group work, I apparently talked over people a lot. Teachers would mention that too—like I needed to let classmates finish or take turns better. I wasn’t trying to be rude, I just would get excited or have a thought and jump in before I lost it.\n\nHomework was a whole thing at home too. My parents used to literally sit next to me while I did it, not necessarily to help with the answers, but to keep me there. If they walked away, I’d drift off and sharpen pencils, or start drawing in the margins, or suddenly decide I needed to organize something, or just wander into another room before I’d actually finished. So yes, it was pretty similar, just in a kid version.\n\nDoctor: These attention problems you’re describing now—do they significantly get in the way of your day-to-day functioning in more than one area of your life, like at work, at home, or in relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. I mean, I can usually sort of hold things together enough that it doesn’t look catastrophic from the outside, but it definitely affects more than just one part of my life.\n\nWork-wise—or I guess freelance-wise, since I’m not in a regular job right now—I’ve lost a couple of small contracts because I didn’t follow through cleanly. Like, I had good ideas and I could do the creative or technical parts, but then I’d miss a message, or forget to send a revision when I said I would, or turn something in kind of messy because I’d lost track of the final details. And once that happens, people don’t really want to keep relying on you, even if they’re nice about it.\n\nThere was also a portfolio competition I really wanted to enter, and I missed out on it because I couldn’t get everything organized and submitted properly. That one really bothered me because it wasn’t that I didn’t care. I cared a lot. I just couldn’t seem to make the follow-through happen in a clean, dependable way.\n\nAnd socially, it affects collaborations and friendships too. Some friends still like my ideas and like brainstorming with me, but I can tell they hesitate to actually work with me on things because they’re not sure I’ll remember what I said I’d do or finish my part smoothly. That feels pretty embarrassing, honestly, because I don’t want to be that person people have to manage.\n\nDoctor: Over the past few months, have you been feeling excessively worried or tense on most days, even when nothing specific is going wrong?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in the way you’re describing, at least. I mean, I do get stressed when I’m behind on something or when I realize I dropped the ball, and then I can kind of spiral a little about the consequences. But it doesn’t feel like I’m just tense or worried most days for no clear reason.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m walking around constantly anxious when nothing is happening. It’s more like the worry shows up when there’s an actual thing I’m dealing with or something I forgot to deal with. If everything is relatively okay, I don’t usually feel excessively worried just out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Over the last few weeks, have you been feeling down, sad, or noticeably less interested or less motivated to do things than is usual for you?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t think I’ve been, like, persistently sad or down in a way that feels unusual for me. I have moments where I feel frustrated or kind of discouraged, especially when I’m not getting things done the way I want to, but I wouldn’t say the last few weeks have felt like a constant low mood.\n\nAs for interest or motivation… that’s a little harder, because my motivation can be kind of inconsistent anyway, so it’s hard for me to tell what’s “less than usual.” I don’t think I’ve noticed a clear drop where suddenly I don’t care about anything or nothing feels worth doing. It’s more that I can get stuck or have trouble starting, but I don’t know if I’d describe that as feeling depressed.\n\nDoctor: Outside of being talkative or interrupting, have you generally found it hard to understand or keep up with social cues—like reading people’s expressions, knowing when someone is bored or joking, or figuring out what’s expected in conversations?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, generally. Like, I don’t usually feel confused by people’s facial expressions or totally miss when someone is joking or bored. I can usually tell, at least in a normal way, if someone’s uncomfortable or if the tone of a conversation changes.\n\nI mean, I might sometimes keep talking too long and then realize a little late that I should wrap it up, but that feels more like I got carried away than like I didn’t understand the cue at all. If someone is being sarcastic or making a joke, I usually get it. And I don’t think I’ve had a pattern of people saying I don’t understand what’s expected socially.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say that’s been a major issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble falling asleep at night, taking a long time to drift off even when you’re trying to sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I definitely have nights where it takes me a while to fall asleep, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s “often” in a way that feels like a main problem. Sometimes I get into bed and I’m trying to sleep, but my mind is still kind of… going through little things, or I’m just not feeling ready to fully shut off yet.\n\nBut it’s not like every night I’m lying there for hours feeling unable to sleep. I haven’t really tracked it, so it’s hard for me to say how frequent it is. I guess I’d say it happens sometimes, but I’m not sure it’s a consistent pattern or something I’ve paid close attention to.\n\nDoctor: In situations where you’re expected to sit still or stay put—like meetings, classes in the past, or long conversations—do you often feel physically restless or find yourself fidgeting, getting up, or feeling like you’re driven by a motor?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. That’s actually one of the things I notice more clearly. If I’m supposed to just sit still through something, like a long Zoom call or when I’m trying to quietly work in one place for a while, I start getting this really uncomfortable feeling in my body. It’s not exactly pain, it’s more like… pressure, or tension, like I need to get up and move around or do something.\n\nSometimes I’ll start shifting positions a lot, or I’ll want to pace for a minute, or I’ll end up messing with something in my hands just to have somewhere for that energy to go. It’s not always that I actually leave the situation, especially if I know I’m supposed to stay put, but internally I feel like I’m kind of fighting the urge to move. It can make sitting through things feel way longer than they probably are.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you’re doing something you genuinely enjoy and find really interesting—like a favorite hobby, game, or show—do you still often find your attention slipping away or feel that same restless, driven feeling in your body?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

Like, I don’t usually feel confused by people’s facial expressions or totally miss when someone is joking or bored. I can usually tell, at least in a normal way, if someone’s uncomfortable or if the tone of a conversation changes.\n\nI mean, I might sometimes keep talking too long and then realize a little late that I should wrap it up, but that feels more like I got carried away than like I didn’t understand the cue at all.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, it can still happen, even if I’m interested in it. Maybe not in the exact same way as when something is boring, but it’s definitely not like interest automatically fixes it.

Like, I can sit down to read a research article that I actually want to understand, or watch a UX tutorial because I’m genuinely interested in design stuff, and for the first few minutes I’m like, okay, I’m doing this, I’m focused. But then I’ll suddenly realize my eyes have been moving across the page, or the video has been playing, and I have basically no idea what I just took in. It’s weird because I’m technically looking at it the whole time, but I’m not really absorbing it.

So then I have to go back and reread the same paragraph, or rewind the tutorial, and sometimes I’ll do that more than once. It’s frustrating because it’s not that I don’t care about the topic. I do. It’s more like my attention slips off without me noticing, and then I come back and realize I missed a chunk.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F32.0, F41.0, F43.2, F51.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s good to meet you. What has been bothering you lately that made you decide to come in today?\n\nPatient: Honestly, the biggest thing is that I get pulled off track really easily. Like, I’ll be working on something I’m supposed to finish—usually a dashboard or some kind of project—and then one tiny thing happens, like a Slack notification pops up, or I hear one line of a song, or I suddenly think of a completely different project idea, and it’s like my brain just… switches channels.\n\nAnd then I’ll realize I’ve totally lost the thread of what I was doing. I’ll be sitting there with the dashboard open, but I don’t remember exactly what part I was fixing or what I was about to check next. It’s frustrating because it’s not like I’m trying to avoid it; I really do mean to stay on task. It just feels like my attention gets grabbed before I can stop it, and then getting back into the original thing takes way more effort than it should. That’s kind of what made me think I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back to when you were a kid in elementary or middle school, did you often have similar trouble staying focused on schoolwork or instructions, even for things you were trying to pay attention to?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, looking back, it was definitely there even when I was little. I didn’t really have words for it then, and I think adults kind of framed it as me being “careless” or “too chatty” or needing to slow down.\n\nIn elementary school, my teachers would write those little report card comments like, “rushes through worksheets,” or “needs to follow directions more carefully,” and I remember getting frustrated because I usually thought I did understand the directions at first. But then I’d start the worksheet too fast, skip a step, or forget partway through what we were supposed to be doing. Sometimes I’d finish something and then realize I’d done, like, the wrong section or missed the part where we were supposed to show work or answer in complete sentences.\n\nAnd with group work, I apparently talked over people a lot. Teachers would mention that too—like I needed to let classmates finish or take turns better. I wasn’t trying to be rude, I just would get excited or have a thought and jump in before I lost it.\n\nHomework was a whole thing at home too. My parents used to literally sit next to me while I did it, not necessarily to help with the answers, but to keep me there. If they walked away, I’d drift off and sharpen pencils, or start drawing in the margins, or suddenly decide I needed to organize something, or just wander into another room before I’d actually finished. So yes, it was pretty similar, just in a kid version.\n\nDoctor: These attention problems you’re describing now—do they significantly get in the way of your day-to-day functioning in more than one area of your life, like at work, at home, or in relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. I mean, I can usually sort of hold things together enough that it doesn’t look catastrophic from the outside, but it definitely affects more than just one part of my life.\n\nWork-wise—or I guess freelance-wise, since I’m not in a regular job right now—I’ve lost a couple of small contracts because I didn’t follow through cleanly. Like, I had good ideas and I could do the creative or technical parts, but then I’d miss a message, or forget to send a revision when I said I would, or turn something in kind of messy because I’d lost track of the final details. And once that happens, people don’t really want to keep relying on you, even if they’re nice about it.\n\nThere was also a portfolio competition I really wanted to enter, and I missed out on it because I couldn’t get everything organized and submitted properly. That one really bothered me because it wasn’t that I didn’t care. I cared a lot. I just couldn’t seem to make the follow-through happen in a clean, dependable way.\n\nAnd socially, it affects collaborations and friendships too. Some friends still like my ideas and like brainstorming with me, but I can tell they hesitate to actually work with me on things because they’re not sure I’ll remember what I said I’d do or finish my part smoothly. That feels pretty embarrassing, honestly, because I don’t want to be that person people have to manage.\n\nDoctor: Over the past few months, have you been feeling excessively worried or tense on most days, even when nothing specific is going wrong?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in the way you’re describing, at least. I mean, I do get stressed when I’m behind on something or when I realize I dropped the ball, and then I can kind of spiral a little about the consequences. But it doesn’t feel like I’m just tense or worried most days for no clear reason.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m walking around constantly anxious when nothing is happening. It’s more like the worry shows up when there’s an actual thing I’m dealing with or something I forgot to deal with. If everything is relatively okay, I don’t usually feel excessively worried just out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Over the last few weeks, have you been feeling down, sad, or noticeably less interested or less motivated to do things than is usual for you?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t think I’ve been, like, persistently sad or down in a way that feels unusual for me. I have moments where I feel frustrated or kind of discouraged, especially when I’m not getting things done the way I want to, but I wouldn’t say the last few weeks have felt like a constant low mood.\n\nAs for interest or motivation… that’s a little harder, because my motivation can be kind of inconsistent anyway, so it’s hard for me to tell what’s “less than usual.” I don’t think I’ve noticed a clear drop where suddenly I don’t care about anything or nothing feels worth doing. It’s more that I can get stuck or have trouble starting, but I don’t know if I’d describe that as feeling depressed.\n\nDoctor: Outside of being talkative or interrupting, have you generally found it hard to understand or keep up with social cues—like reading people’s expressions, knowing when someone is bored or joking, or figuring out what’s expected in conversations?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, generally. Like, I don’t usually feel confused by people’s facial expressions or totally miss when someone is joking or bored. I can usually tell, at least in a normal way, if someone’s uncomfortable or if the tone of a conversation changes.\n\nI mean, I might sometimes keep talking too long and then realize a little late that I should wrap it up, but that feels more like I got carried away than like I didn’t understand the cue at all. If someone is being sarcastic or making a joke, I usually get it. And I don’t think I’ve had a pattern of people saying I don’t understand what’s expected socially.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say that’s been a major issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble falling asleep at night, taking a long time to drift off even when you’re trying to sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. Sometimes I get into bed and I’m trying to sleep, but my mind is still kind of… going through little things, or I’m just not feeling ready to fully shut off yet.\n\nBut it’s not like every night I’m lying there for hours feeling unable to sleep. It’s not always that I actually leave the situation, especially if I know I’m supposed to stay put, but internally I feel like I’m kind of fighting the urge to move. It can make sitting through things feel way longer than they probably are.\n\nDoctor: When you’re doing something you genuinely enjoy and find really interesting—like a favorite hobby, game, or show—do you still often find your attention slipping away or feel that same restless, driven feeling in your body?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it can still happen, even if I’m interested in it. But then I’ll suddenly realize my eyes have been moving across the page, or the video has been playing, and I have basically no idea what I just took in. It’s weird because I’m technically looking at it the whole time, but I’m not really absorbing it.\n\nSo then I have to go back and reread the same paragraph, or rewind the tutorial, and sometimes I’ll do that more than once. Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\"key\":\"S008\",\"name\":\"Distractibility\",\"description\":\"Often easily distracted by extraneous stimuli (for older adolescents and adults, may include unrelated thoughts).\",\"reason\":\"This is the first reply of the consultation, so I am returning the chief complaint symptom code as instructed.\"}\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.   }
]

Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event. Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI’m not really sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t think I’ve been, like, persistently sad or down in a way that feels unusual for me. I have moments where I feel frustrated or kind of discouraged, especially when I’m not getting things done the way I want to, but I wouldn’t say the last few weeks have felt like a constant low mood.\n\nAs for interest or motivation… that’s a little harder, because my motivation can be kind of inconsistent anyway, so it’s hard for me to tell what’s “less than usual.” I don’t think I’ve noticed a clear drop where suddenly I don’t care about anything or nothing feels worth doing. It’s more that I can get stuck or have trouble starting, but I don’t know if I’d describe that as feeling depressed.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }

Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI’m not totally sure. I mean, I definitely have nights where it takes me a while to fall asleep, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s “often” in a way that feels like a main problem. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.
